From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

Bilateral relations

__Iran–United States relations__
| [](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Iran%5FUnited%5FStates%5FLocator%5F2.svg "Map indicating locations of Iran and United States")                   |                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                        |

| [Interests Section of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interests%5FSection%5Fof%5FIran%5Fin%5Fthe%5FUnited%5FStates "Interests Section of Iran in the United States") in the [Embassy of Pakistan, Washington, D.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Embassy%5Fof%5FPakistan,%5FWashington,%5FD.C. "Embassy of Pakistan, Washington, D.C.") | [Interests Section of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Embassy%5Fof%5Fthe%5FUnited%5FStates,%5FTehran#U.S.%5FInterests%5FSection%5Fof%5Fthe%5FSwiss%5FEmbassy "Embassy of the United States, Tehran") in the [Embassy of Switzerland, Tehran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran–Switzerland%5Frelations "Iran–Switzerland relations") |

[](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:American–Iranian%5FWaving%5FFlag%5FIcon.svg)

[American flag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American%5Fflag "American flag") crossed with the [Iranian opposition flag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lion%5Fand%5FSun%5Fflag "Lion and Sun flag")

Relations between [Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran "Iran") and the [United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United%5FStates "United States") began in the mid-19th century, when Iran was known to the [Western world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western%5Fworld "Western world") as [Qajar Persia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qajar%5FPersia "Qajar Persia"). While Persia was the object of British and Russian colonial interests during the [Great Game](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great%5FGame "Great Game"), the US gained the trust of its leaders, with [Arthur Millspaugh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur%5FMillspaugh "Arthur Millspaugh") and [Morgan Shuster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morgan%5FShuster "Morgan Shuster") appointed treasurers-general by the [Shahs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shah%5Fof%5FIran "Shah of Iran"). During World War II, Iran was [invaded](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Soviet%5Finvasion%5Fof%5FIran "Anglo-Soviet invasion of Iran") by the [United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United%5FKingdom "United Kingdom") and the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet%5FUnion "Soviet Union"), both U.S. allies. Relations remained positive until the [Mohammad Mosaddegh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad%5FMosaddegh "Mohammad Mosaddegh") government was overthrown by the [1953 Iranian coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1953%5FIranian%5Fcoup%5Fd'état "1953 Iranian coup d'état"), organized by the [Central Intelligence Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central%5FIntelligence%5FAgency "Central Intelligence Agency"), and assisted by [MI6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MI6 "MI6"). This was followed by an alliance between Shah [Mohammad Reza Pahlavi's regime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pahlavi%5FIran "Pahlavi Iran") and the US government.[\[1\]](#cite%5Fnote-Ansari%5F2006-1) From being close allies during the [Cold War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cold%5FWar "Cold War"),[\[2\]](#cite%5Fnote-2)[\[3\]](#cite%5Fnote-3)[\[4\]](#cite%5Fnote-4) the Iran-US relations flipped after the 1979 [Iranian Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian%5FRevolution "Iranian Revolution"), when Ayatollah [Ruhollah Khomeini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruhollah%5FKhomeini "Ruhollah Khomeini") took power.[\[1\]](#cite%5Fnote-Ansari%5F2006-1)[\[5\]](#cite%5Fnote-ABC-CLIO-5)

The two nations have had no formal [diplomatic relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diplomatic%5Frelations "Diplomatic relations") since 1980:[\[1\]](#cite%5Fnote-Ansari%5F2006-1) [Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan "Pakistan") and [Switzerland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland "Switzerland") serving as [protecting powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protecting%5Fpower "Protecting power") for Iran and the US respectively. Communication goes through the [Pakistani Embassy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interests%5FSection%5Fof%5FIran%5Fin%5Fthe%5FUnited%5FStates "Interests Section of Iran in the United States") in [Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington,%5FD.C. "Washington, D.C.")[\[6\]](#cite%5Fnote-6) and the [Swiss Embassy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Embassy%5Fof%5Fthe%5FUnited%5FStates,%5FTehran#U.S.%5FInterests%5FSection%5Fof%5Fthe%5FSwiss%5FEmbassy "Embassy of the United States, Tehran") in [Tehran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tehran "Tehran").[\[7\]](#cite%5Fnote-7) Since 1995, the United States has had an [embargo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Embargo "Embargo") on trade with Iran.[\[8\]](#cite%5Fnote-8) Relations improved when the two countries had overlapping goals, such as joining forces in the [war against the Islamic State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War%5Fagainst%5Fthe%5FIslamic%5FState "War against the Islamic State").[\[9\]](#cite%5Fnote-9) In 2015, the [Joint Comprehensive Plan of Action](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint%5FComprehensive%5FPlan%5Fof%5FAction "Joint Comprehensive Plan of Action") to limit Iran's nuclear program was signed; most [sanctions against Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanctions%5Fagainst%5FIran "Sanctions against Iran") were lifted the next year.[\[10\]](#cite%5Fnote-10)[\[11\]](#cite%5Fnote-11)[\[12\]](#cite%5Fnote-12) The first Trump administration [withdrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United%5FStates%5Fwithdrawal%5Ffrom%5Fthe%5FJoint%5FComprehensive%5FPlan%5Fof%5FAction "United States withdrawal from the Joint Comprehensive Plan of Action") from the deal and [re-imposed sanctions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maximum%5Fpressure%5Fcampaign "Maximum pressure campaign") in 2018;[\[13\]](#cite%5Fnote-13) in August, former Supreme leader [Ali Khamenei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali%5FKhamenei "Ali Khamenei") banned direct talks with the United States.[\[14\]](#cite%5Fnote-Staff-14) In the years that followed Iran reduced its deal commitments and exceeded pre-JCPOA enrichment levels.[\[15\]](#cite%5Fnote-15) The [Biden administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biden%5Fadministration "Biden administration") increased sanctions; so did the [second Trump administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second%5FTrump%5Fadministration "Second Trump administration").

On February 28, 2026, [Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel "Israel") and the United States launched a [joint attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List%5Fof%5Fattacks%5Fduring%5Fthe%5F2026%5FIran%5Fwar "List of attacks during the 2026 Iran war") on various sites in Iran, beginning [a war between the three countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2026%5FIran%5Fwar "2026 Iran war"). The operation included the [assassination of Ali Khamenei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assassination%5Fof%5FAli%5FKhamenei "Assassination of Ali Khamenei"), Iran's supreme leader.[\[16\]](#cite%5Fnote-16) After three months of war, the US and Iran announced on June 14, 2026, that they had agreed to halt hostilities. Mediated by Pakistan and [Qatar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qatar "Qatar"), the [memorandum of understanding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamabad%5FMemorandum "Islamabad Memorandum") presented a 60-day extension of the existing ceasefire to lay the groundwork for a final settlement.[\[17\]](#cite%5Fnote-axios-mou-17)[\[18\]](#cite%5Fnote-wapo-framework-18)

## History

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Iran%E2%80%93United%5FStates%5Frelations&action=edit&section=1%20%22Edit%20section:%20History%22)\]

### Early relations

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Iran%E2%80%93United%5FStates%5Frelations&action=edit&section=2%20%22Edit%20section:%20Early%20relations%22)\]

[American newspapers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History%5Fof%5FAmerican%5Fnewspapers#Colonial%5Fperiod "History of American newspapers") in the 1720s were uniformly pro-Iranian, especially during the revolt of Afghan emir [Mahmud Hotak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahmud%5FHotak "Mahmud Hotak") (r. 1722–1725) against the [Safavid dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safavid%5Fdynasty "Safavid dynasty").[\[19\]](#cite%5Fnote-19)

Political relations between [Qajar Persia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qajar%5FPersia "Qajar Persia") and the [United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United%5FStates "United States") began when the [Shah of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shah%5Fof%5FIran "Shah of Iran"), [Nassereddin Shah Qajar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nassereddin%5FShah%5FQajar "Nassereddin Shah Qajar"), officially dispatched Iran's first ambassador, Mirza Abolhasan, to [Washington, D.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington,%5FD.C. "Washington, D.C.") in 1856.[\[20\]](#cite%5Fnote-PR-20) In 1883, [Samuel G. W. Benjamin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel%5FG.%5FW.%5FBenjamin "Samuel G. W. Benjamin") was appointed by the United States as the first official diplomatic envoy to Iran; however, ambassadorial relations were not established until 1944.[\[20\]](#cite%5Fnote-PR-20)

* [](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Arg-Tabriz-USA.jpg "The U.S. Consulate at Arg e Tabriz sits in the line of fire during the Iranian Constitutional Revolution. While the city was being attacked and bombed by 4,000 Russian troops in December 1911, Howard Baskerville took to arms, helping the people of Iran.")  
The U.S. Consulate at [Arg e Tabriz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arg%5Fe%5FTabriz "Arg e Tabriz") sits in the line of fire during the [Iranian Constitutional Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian%5FConstitutional%5FRevolution "Iranian Constitutional Revolution"). While the city was being attacked and bombed by 4,000 Russian troops in December 1911, [Howard Baskerville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Howard%5FBaskerville "Howard Baskerville") took to arms, helping the people of Iran.
* [](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Americans%5Fwearing%5FPersian%5Fclothes.jpg "Americans wearing jobbeh va kolah (traditional Persian clothes) at the opening of the Majles, January 29, 1924. Mr. McCaskey, Dr. Arthur Millspaugh, and Colonel MacCormack are seen in the photo.")  
Americans wearing _jobbeh va kolah_ (traditional Persian clothes) at the opening of the _[Majles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majles "Majles")_, January 29, 1924\. Mr. McCaskey, Dr. [Arthur Millspaugh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur%5FMillspaugh "Arthur Millspaugh"), and Colonel MacCormack are seen in the photo.
* [](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Shuster-team.jpg "Morgan Shuster and U.S. officials at Atabak Palace, Tehran, 1911. Their group was appointed by Iran's parliament to reform and modernize Iran's Department of Treasury and Finances.")  
[Morgan Shuster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morgan%5FShuster "Morgan Shuster") and U.S. officials at Atabak Palace, [Tehran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tehran "Tehran"), 1911\. Their group was appointed by [Iran's parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majles "Majles") to reform and modernize Iran's Department of Treasury and Finances.
* [](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:American%5FCollege%5FTehran.JPG "McCormick Hall, American College of Tehran, circa 1930, chartered by the State University of New York in 1932. Americans also founded Iran's first modern College of Medicine in the 1870s.")  
McCormick Hall, [American College of Tehran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alborz%5FHigh%5FSchool "Alborz High School"), circa 1930, chartered by the [State University of New York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State%5FUniversity%5Fof%5FNew%5FYork "State University of New York") in 1932\. Americans also founded [Iran's first modern College of Medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urmia%5FUniversity "Urmia University") in the 1870s.
* [](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Joseph%5FPlumb%5FCochran.jpeg "Joseph Plumb Cochran, American Presbyterian missionary. He is credited as the founder of Iran's first modern medical school.")  
[Joseph Plumb Cochran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph%5FCochran "Joseph Cochran"), American Presbyterian missionary. He is credited as the founder of Iran's first modern medical school.
* [](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Tabriz%5Fmemorial%5Fhighschool%5F00.jpg "American Memorial School in Tabriz, established in 1881")  
[American Memorial School in Tabriz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American%5FMemorial%5FSchool%5Fin%5FTabriz "American Memorial School in Tabriz"), established in 1881

The U.S. had little interest in [Persian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran "Iran") affairs, while the U.S. as a trustworthy outsider did not suffer. The Persians again sought the U.S. for help in straightening out its finances after [World War I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World%5FWar%5FI "World War I"). This mission was opposed by powerful vested interests and eventually was withdrawn with its task incomplete.[\[21\]](#cite%5Fnote-21)

During the [Persian Constitutional Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian%5FConstitutional%5FRevolution "Persian Constitutional Revolution") in 1909, American [Howard Baskerville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Howard%5FBaskerville "Howard Baskerville") died in [Tabriz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tabriz "Tabriz") while fighting with a militia in a battle against royalist forces.[\[22\]](#cite%5Fnote-22) After the Iranian parliament appointed United States financier [Morgan Shuster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morgan%5FShuster "Morgan Shuster") as Treasurer General of Iran in 1911, an American was killed in [Tehran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tehran "Tehran") by gunmen thought to be affiliated with Russian or British interests. Shuster became even more active in supporting the Constitutional Revolution of Iran financially.[\[23\]](#cite%5Fnote-23)

[Arthur Millspaugh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur%5FMillspaugh "Arthur Millspaugh"), an American economic adviser, was sent to Persia in 1923 as a private citizen to help reform its inefficient administration. His presence was seen by Persians as a means to attract foreign investment and counterbalance European influence. The mission ended in 1928 after Millspaugh fell out of favor with the shah.[\[24\]](#cite%5Fnote-delv-24)

Until World War II, relations between Iran and the United States remained cordial. As a result, many Iranians sympathetic to the [Persian Constitutional Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian%5FConstitutional%5FRevolution "Persian Constitutional Revolution") came to view the U.S. as a "third force" in their struggle to expel British and Russian dominance in Persian affairs. American industrial and business leaders were supportive of Iran's drive to modernize its economy and to expel British and Russian influence from the country.[\[25\]](#cite%5Fnote-25)

[Reza Khan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reza%5FKhan "Reza Khan"), a military officer in Persia's [Cossack Brigade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian%5FCossack%5FBrigade "Persian Cossack Brigade"), came to power after leading a British-backed coup in 1921 that overthrew the [Qajar dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qajar%5Fdynasty "Qajar dynasty").[\[26\]](#cite%5Fnote-26)[\[27\]](#cite%5Fnote-27) He later declared himself [shah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shah "Shah") (king) and took the name Reza Pahlavi.[\[28\]](#cite%5Fnote-weisse-28) He launched modernization efforts, building a national railway and introducing secular education, while also censoring the press, suppressing unions and parties, and later banning the hijab in favor of Western dress.[\[28\]](#cite%5Fnote-weisse-28)In 1936, Iran withdrew its ambassador in Washington for nearly one year after the publication of an article criticizing Reza Shah in _[The New York Daily Herald](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The%5FNew%5FYork%5FHerald "The New York Herald")_.[\[29\]](#cite%5Fnote-29)

[](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Teheran%5Fconference-1943.jpg)

The [Allied](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allies%5Fof%5FWorld%5FWar%5FII "Allies of World War II") "Big Three" at the 1943 [Tehran Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tehran%5FConference "Tehran Conference")

After a dispute over German influence during [World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World%5FWar%5FII "World War II"), Reza Shah was forced to abdicate in 1941 and was succeeded by his son, [Mohammad Reza Pahlavi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad%5FReza%5FPahlavi "Mohammad Reza Pahlavi").[\[28\]](#cite%5Fnote-weisse-28) During the rest of World War II, Iran became [a major conduit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian%5FCorridor "Persian Corridor") for [British and American aid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lend-Lease "Lend-Lease") to the Soviet Union and an avenue through which [over 120,000 Polish refugees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evacuation%5Fof%5FPolish%5Fcivilians%5Ffrom%5Fthe%5FUSSR%5Fin%5FWorld%5FWar%5FII#Iran%5Fand%5Fthe%5FMiddle%5FEast "Evacuation of Polish civilians from the USSR in World War II") and [Polish Armed Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anders'%5FArmy "Anders' Army") fled the Axis advance.[\[30\]](#cite%5Fnote-30) At the 1943 [Tehran Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tehran%5FConference "Tehran Conference"), the [Allied](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allies%5Fof%5FWorld%5FWar%5FII "Allies of World War II") "Big Three"—[Joseph Stalin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph%5FStalin "Joseph Stalin"), [Franklin D. Roosevelt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franklin%5FD.%5FRoosevelt "Franklin D. Roosevelt"), and [Winston Churchill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winston%5FChurchill "Winston Churchill")—issued the [Tehran Declaration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tehran%5FConference#Three%5Fpowers%5Fcome%5Ftogether "Tehran Conference") to guarantee the post-war independence and boundaries of Iran. In 1949, the [Constituent Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constituent%5FAssembly "Constituent Assembly") of Iran gave the shah the power to dissolve the parliament.[\[28\]](#cite%5Fnote-weisse-28)

### 1953 Iranian coup d'état

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Iran%E2%80%93United%5FStates%5Frelations&action=edit&section=3%20%22Edit%20section:%201953%20Iranian%20coup%20d%27%C3%A9tat%22)\]

#### Before the coup

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Iran%E2%80%93United%5FStates%5Frelations&action=edit&section=4%20%22Edit%20section:%20Before%20the%20coup%22)\]

[](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Shah%5F&%5FHarry%5FTruman.jpg)

The Shah with [Harry S. Truman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harry%5FS.%5FTruman "Harry S. Truman") in 1949

Until the outbreak of World War II, the United States had no active policy toward Iran.[\[31\]](#cite%5Fnote-31) When the [Cold War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cold%5FWar "Cold War") began, the United States was alarmed by the attempt by the Soviet Union to set up separatist states in Iranian Azerbaijan and Kurdistan, as well as its demand for military rights to the [Dardanelles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dardanelles "Dardanelles") in 1946\. This fear was enhanced by the [loss of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loss%5Fof%5FChina "Loss of China") to communism, the uncovering of Soviet spy rings, and the start of the [Korean War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean%5FWar "Korean War").[\[32\]](#cite%5Fnote-32)

#### Prime minister Mossadeq

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Iran%E2%80%93United%5FStates%5Frelations&action=edit&section=5%20%22Edit%20section:%20Prime%20minister%20Mossadeq%22)\]

In 1951, Iran [nationalised its oil industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nationalization%5Fof%5Fthe%5FIranian%5Foil%5Findustry "Nationalization of the Iranian oil industry"),[\[33\]](#cite%5Fnote-33) effectively seizing the assets of the [Anglo-Iranian Oil Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglo-Iranian%5FOil%5FCompany "Anglo-Iranian Oil Company") (AIOC).[\[34\]](#cite%5Fnote-34) On April 28, 1951, [Mohammad Mosaddegh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad%5FMosaddegh "Mohammad Mosaddegh") was elected as Prime Minister by the [Parliament of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament%5Fof%5FIran "Parliament of Iran").

The British planned to retaliate by attacking Iran,[\[35\]](#cite%5Fnote-35) but U.S. President [Harry S. Truman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency%5Fof%5FHarry%5FS.%5FTruman "Presidency of Harry S. Truman") pressed Britain to moderate its position in the negotiations and to not invade Iran. American policies fostered a sense in Iran that the United States supported Mossadeq, along with optimism that the oil dispute would soon be resolved through "a series of innovative proposals" that would provide Iran with "significant amounts of economic aid." Mossadeq visited Washington, and the American government made "frequent statements expressing support for him."[\[36\]](#cite%5Fnote-36)

At the same time, the United States honored the British embargo and, without Truman's knowledge, the Central Intelligence Agency station in Tehran had been "carrying out covert activities" against Mosaddeq and the National Front "at least since the summer of 1952".[\[37\]](#cite%5Fnote-37)

#### The coup d'état

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Iran%E2%80%93United%5FStates%5Frelations&action=edit&section=6%20%22Edit%20section:%20The%20coup%20d%27%C3%A9tat%22)\]

Main article: [1953 Iranian coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1953%5FIranian%5Fcoup%5Fd'état "1953 Iranian coup d'état")

[](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:ShahEisenhower.jpg)

The Shah with [Dwight D. Eisenhower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dwight%5FD.%5FEisenhower "Dwight D. Eisenhower") in 1959

In 1953, the U.S. and [Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United%5FKingdom "United Kingdom") orchestrated a [coup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1953%5FIranian%5Fcoup%5Fd'état "1953 Iranian coup d'état") to overthrow Iran's prime minister [Mohammad Mosaddeq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad%5FMosaddeq "Mohammad Mosaddeq"),[\[38\]](#cite%5Fnote-briefhis-38) fearing communist influence and economic instability after Iran nationalized its oil industry. The coup, led by the [CIA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CIA "CIA") and [MI6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MI6 "MI6"),[\[38\]](#cite%5Fnote-briefhis-38) initially failed but succeeded on a second attempt. It reinstalled [Shah Mohammad Reza Pahlavi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shah%5FMohammad%5FReza%5FPahlavi "Shah Mohammad Reza Pahlavi"), who then received substantial U.S. financial and military support. The U.S. helped establish [SAVAK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SAVAK "SAVAK"),[\[28\]](#cite%5Fnote-weisse-28) the Shah's brutal secret police, to maintain his rule. Many liberal Iranians believe that the coup and the subsequent U.S. support for the shah enabled the Shah's arbitrary rule, contributing to the "deeply anti-American character" of the [1979 revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1979%5Frevolution "1979 revolution") later.[\[39\]](#cite%5Fnote-39)

After the coup, the United States played a central role in reorganizing Iran's oil sector. Under U.S. pressure, [BP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BP "BP") joined a [consortium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consortium%5FAgreement%5Fof%5F1954 "Consortium Agreement of 1954") of Western companies to resume Iranian oil exports.[\[40\]](#cite%5Fnote-Ref-B-Vassiliou2009-40)[\[41\]](#cite%5Fnote-Ref-B-Lauterpacht1973-41) The consortium operated on behalf of the state-owned National Iranian Oil Company (NIOC), which retained formal ownership of Iran's oil and infrastructure.[\[40\]](#cite%5Fnote-Ref-B-Vassiliou2009-40)[\[41\]](#cite%5Fnote-Ref-B-Lauterpacht1973-41) While Iran received 50% of the profits, U.S. companies collectively secured 40% of the remaining share.[\[42\]](#cite%5Fnote-Ref-B-Christine2008-42) However, the consortium maintained operational control, barred Iranian oversight of its financial records, and excluded Iranians from its board.[\[43\]](#cite%5Fnote-Stephen2003-43) The agreement was part of a wider transition from British to American dominance in the region and worldwide.[\[44\]](#cite%5Fnote-Pluto%5FPress-44)

While initially seen as a [Cold War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cold%5FWar "Cold War") success, the coup later became a source of deep resentment, with critics calling it a blow to democracy and a lasting stain on U.S.-Iran relations.

The Shah with [John F. Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John%5FF.%5FKennedy "John F. Kennedy") and [Robert McNamara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert%5FMcNamara "Robert McNamara") in 1961

#### Nuclear cooperation

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Iran%E2%80%93United%5FStates%5Frelations&action=edit&section=8%20%22Edit%20section:%20Nuclear%20cooperation%22)\]

In 1953, U.S. President [Eisenhower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eisenhower "Eisenhower") launched Iran's nuclear program as part of [Atoms for Peace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atoms%5Ffor%5FPeace "Atoms for Peace") program.[\[45\]](#cite%5Fnote-MA-45)[\[46\]](#cite%5Fnote-46) In 1967, the U.S. helped Iran create its nuclear program by providing the Tehran Research Reactor, the nation's first [nuclear reactor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear%5Freactor "Nuclear reactor"), as well as 5.58 kg of [enriched uranium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enriched%5Furanium "Enriched uranium").[\[47\]](#cite%5Fnote-47)[\[48\]](#cite%5Fnote-48)[\[45\]](#cite%5Fnote-MA-45)[\[49\]](#cite%5Fnote-49) Iran is one of the 51 original signatories of the Treaty on the Non-Proliferation of Nuclear Weapons (NPT) on July 1, 1968, and its Parliament ratified the treaty in February 1970.[\[24\]](#cite%5Fnote-delv-24) The participation of the U.S. and Western European governments continued until the 1979 [Iranian Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian%5FRevolution "Iranian Revolution").[\[50\]](#cite%5Fnote-50)

#### Strategic alliance and geopolitical importance

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Iran%E2%80%93United%5FStates%5Frelations&action=edit&section=9%20%22Edit%20section:%20Strategic%20alliance%20and%20geopolitical%20importance%22)\]

President Lyndon Johnson welcoming the Shah at the White House while Iranians protest, 1967

Iran's border with the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet%5FUnion "Soviet Union"), and its position as the largest, most powerful country in the oil-rich Persian Gulf, made Iran a "pillar" of [U.S. foreign policy in the Middle East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S.%5Fforeign%5Fpolicy%5Fin%5Fthe%5FMiddle%5FEast "U.S. foreign policy in the Middle East").[\[51\]](#cite%5Fnote-51) In 1960, Iran joined [Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraq "Iraq"), [Kuwait](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuwait "Kuwait"), [Saudi Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saudi%5FArabia "Saudi Arabia"), and [Venezuela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venezuela "Venezuela") to form the [Organization of Petroleum Exporting Countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organization%5Fof%5FPetroleum%5FExporting%5FCountries "Organization of Petroleum Exporting Countries") (OPEC), aiming to challenge the dominance of Western oil companies and reclaim control over national oil resources.[\[52\]](#cite%5Fnote-Kalir-52) In the 1960s and 1970s, Iran's oil revenues grew considerably. Beginning in the mid-1960s, this development "weakened U.S. influence in Iranian politics" while strengthening the Iranian state's power over its own population.\[_[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation%5Fneeded "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\] By the 1970s, surging OPEC profits gave the group substantial leverage over Western economies and elevated Iran's strategic value as a U.S. ally.[\[52\]](#cite%5Fnote-Kalir-52) According to scholar [Homa Katouzian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homa%5FKatouzian "Homa Katouzian"), this put the United States "in the contradictory position of being regarded" by the Iranian public "as the chief architect and instructor of the regime," while "its real influence" in domestic Iranian politics and policies "declined considerably".[\[53\]](#cite%5Fnote-53)

#### Military cooperation and arms sales

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Iran%E2%80%93United%5FStates%5Frelations&action=edit&section=10%20%22Edit%20section:%20Military%20cooperation%20and%20arms%20sales%22)\]

Lee Driscoll and other historians have said that between 1969 and 1974 U.S. President [Richard Nixon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard%5FNixon "Richard Nixon") actively recruited the Shah as an American puppet and proxy.[\[54\]](#cite%5Fnote-54) However, Richard Alvandi argues that it worked the other way around, with the Shah taking the initiative. President Nixon, who had first met the Shah in 1953, regarded him as a westernizing anticommunist statesman who deserved American support now that the British were withdrawing from the region. They met again in 1972 and the Shah agreed to buy large quantities of American military hardware, and took responsibility for ensuring political stability and fighting off Soviet subversion throughout the region.[\[55\]](#cite%5Fnote-55) Permitting Iran to purchase U.S. arms served [Cold War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cold%5FWar "Cold War") objectives by securing the Shah's alignment with Washington after Iran had briefly explored Soviet alternatives in the 1960s, while also benefiting the American economy.[\[56\]](#cite%5Fnote-blankch-56) However, because of the [1973 Arab–Israeli War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1973%5FArab–Israeli%5FWar "1973 Arab–Israeli War") and the subsequent [Arab oil embargo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab%5Foil%5Fembargo "Arab oil embargo") against the United States, oil prices became very high. This enabled the shah to buy more advanced weaponry than U.S. officials had expected, which caused concern in Washington.[\[52\]](#cite%5Fnote-Kalir-52)

In the 1970s, approximately 25,000 American technicians were deployed to Iran to maintain military equipment (such as [F-14s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grumman%5FF-14%5FTomcat "Grumman F-14 Tomcat")) that had been sold to the Shah's government.[\[57\]](#cite%5Fnote-57)

#### Cultural and academic relations

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Iran%E2%80%93United%5FStates%5Frelations&action=edit&section=11%20%22Edit%20section:%20Cultural%20and%20academic%20relations%22)\]

[](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:The%5FShah%5Fof%5FIran%5Fand%5FPresident%5FNixon%5F-%5FNARA%5F-%5F194301.jpg)

The Shah with [Richard Nixon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard%5FNixon "Richard Nixon") in 1969

Cultural relations between the two countries remained cordial until 1979\. [Pahlavi University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pahlavi%5FUniversity "Pahlavi University"), [Sharif University of Technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sharif%5FUniversity%5Fof%5FTechnology "Sharif University of Technology"), and [Isfahan University of Technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isfahan%5FUniversity%5Fof%5FTechnology "Isfahan University of Technology"), three of Iran's top academic universities, were directly modeled on [private](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Private%5Funiversity "Private university") American institutions such as the [University of Chicago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University%5Fof%5FChicago "University of Chicago"), [MIT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MIT "MIT"), and the [University of Pennsylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University%5Fof%5FPennsylvania "University of Pennsylvania").[\[58\]](#cite%5Fnote-GE-58)[\[59\]](#cite%5Fnote-59) The Shah was generous in awarding American universities with financial gifts. For example, the [University of Southern California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University%5Fof%5FSouthern%5FCalifornia "University of Southern California") received an endowed chair of petroleum engineering, and a million-dollar donation was given to the [George Washington University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George%5FWashington%5FUniversity "George Washington University") to create an [Iranian Studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranistics "Iranistics") program.[\[58\]](#cite%5Fnote-GE-58)

Prior to the Iranian Revolution of 1979, many Iranian citizens, especially students, resided in the United States and had a positive and welcoming attitude toward America and Americans.[\[5\]](#cite%5Fnote-ABC-CLIO-5) From 1950 to 1979, an estimated 800,000 to 850,000 Americans had visited or lived in Iran, and had often expressed their admiration for the Iranian people.[\[5\]](#cite%5Fnote-ABC-CLIO-5)

### Ford administration 1974–1977

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Iran%E2%80%93United%5FStates%5Frelations&action=edit&section=12%20%22Edit%20section:%20Ford%20administration%201974%E2%80%931977%22)\]

Under president [Gerald Ford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerald%5FFord "Gerald Ford"), U.S.–Iran relations began to cool. Unlike Richard Nixon, Ford lacked a close personal relationship with the Shah, and his administration took a more cautious stance on nuclear cooperation.[\[60\]](#cite%5Fnote-bic-60) Negotiations over American nuclear exports to Iran faltered as Ford insisted on additional safeguards beyond those required by the Nuclear [Non-Proliferation Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-Proliferation%5FTreaty "Non-Proliferation Treaty"), which the Shah rejected as an infringement on Iran's sovereignty.[\[61\]](#cite%5Fnote-oraox-61)

In 1975, President Ford approved a plan allowing Iran to process U.S. nuclear materials and purchase a plutonium reprocessing facility. [Henry Kissinger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry%5FKissinger "Henry Kissinger") later described it as a commercial deal with an ally, with no discussion of weapons concerns.[\[24\]](#cite%5Fnote-delv-24)

Despite continuing Nixon's arms sales policy, granting Iran wide access to U.S. weapons, Ford faced internal opposition and growing concerns in Congress.[\[56\]](#cite%5Fnote-blankch-56) Meanwhile, U.S. officials—particularly Treasury Secretary [William Simon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William%5FE.%5FSimon "William E. Simon") grew increasingly critical of the Shah's role in maintaining high oil prices, during a time when surging inflation was driving the U.S. economy toward a recession. To America, the Shah's strategy of pumping oil profits into domestic investments, such as its military, indicated Iran's desire for superpower status.[\[62\]](#cite%5Fnote-62)[\[63\]](#cite%5Fnote-63) In 1976, the U.S. covertly supported Saudi Arabia's move to drive down oil prices, undercutting Iran's revenues.[\[60\]](#cite%5Fnote-bic-60)

The resulting oil price collapse in early 1977 triggered a severe financial crisis in Iran, forcing austerity measures that led to rising unemployment and social unrest. These developments significantly weakened the Shah's regime and contributed to the conditions that precipitated the 1979 [Islamic Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic%5FRevolution "Islamic Revolution"). Later declassified documents suggest that key U.S. policymakers underestimated the risks of destabilizing their long-time ally.[\[60\]](#cite%5Fnote-bic-60)

### Carter administration 1977–1981

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Iran%E2%80%93United%5FStates%5Frelations&action=edit&section=13%20%22Edit%20section:%20Carter%20administration%201977%E2%80%931981%22)\]

See also: [Jimmy Carter's engagement with Ruhollah Khomeini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimmy%5FCarter's%5Fengagement%5Fwith%5FRuhollah%5FKhomeini "Jimmy Carter's engagement with Ruhollah Khomeini")

[](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:The%5FShah%5Fwith%5FAtherton,%5FSullivan,%5FVance,%5FCarter%5Fand%5FBrzezinski,%5F1977.jpg)

The Iranian Shah, Mohammad Reza Pahlavi meeting with [Alfred Atherton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfred%5FAtherton "Alfred Atherton"), [William H. Sullivan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William%5FH.%5FSullivan "William H. Sullivan"), [Cyrus Vance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyrus%5FVance "Cyrus Vance"), President [Jimmy Carter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimmy%5FCarter "Jimmy Carter"), and [Zbigniew Brzezinski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zbigniew%5FBrzezinski "Zbigniew Brzezinski"), 1977

[](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:DF-SC-86-12761.jpg)

[Mohammed Reza Pahlavi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammed%5FReza%5FPahlavi "Mohammed Reza Pahlavi"), [Shah of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pahlavi%5Fdynasty "Pahlavi dynasty"), shakes hands with a [U.S. Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S.%5FAir%5FForce "U.S. Air Force") general officer prior to his departure from the United States

In the late 1970s, American President [Jimmy Carter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimmy%5FCarter "Jimmy Carter") emphasized human rights in his foreign policy, but went easy in private with the Shah.[\[64\]](#cite%5Fnote-64) By 1977, Iran had garnered unfavorable publicity in the international community for its bad human rights record.[\[65\]](#cite%5Fnote-65) That year, the Shah responded to Carter's "polite reminder" by granting amnesty to some prisoners and allowing the [Red Cross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International%5FRed%5FCross%5Fand%5FRed%5FCrescent%5FMovement "International Red Cross and Red Crescent Movement") to visit prisons. Through 1977, liberal opposition formed organizations and issued open letters denouncing the Shah's regime.[\[66\]](#cite%5Fnote-66)[\[67\]](#cite%5Fnote-JC-67)

Carter angered anti-Shah Iranians with a New Year's Eve 1978 toast to the Shah in which he said:

> Under the Shah's brilliant leadership Iran is an island of stability in one of the most troublesome regions of the world. There is no other state figure whom I could appreciate and like more.[\[68\]](#cite%5Fnote-68)

Observers disagree over the nature of United States policy toward Iran under Carter as the Shah's regime crumbled. According to historian [Nikki Keddie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikki%5FKeddie "Nikki Keddie"), the Carter administration followed "no clear policy" on Iran.[\[69\]](#cite%5Fnote-69) The [U.S. National Security Advisor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S.%5FNational%5FSecurity%5FAdvisor "U.S. National Security Advisor") [Zbigniew Brzezinski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zbigniew%5FBrzezinski "Zbigniew Brzezinski") "repeatedly assured Pahlavi that the U.S. backed him fully". At the same time, officials in the State Department believed the revolution was unstoppable.[\[70\]](#cite%5Fnote-70) After visiting the Shah in 1978, Secretary of the Treasury [W. Michael Blumenthal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/W.%5FMichael%5FBlumenthal "W. Michael Blumenthal") complained of the Shah's emotional collapse.[\[71\]](#cite%5Fnote-71) Brzezinski and [Energy Secretary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US%5FSecretary%5Fof%5FEnergy "US Secretary of Energy") [James Schlesinger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James%5FSchlesinger "James Schlesinger") were adamant in assurances that the Shah would receive military support.

Sociologist [Charles Kurzman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles%5FKurzman "Charles Kurzman") argues that the Carter administration was consistently supportive of the Shah and urged the Iranian military to stage a "last-resort coup d'état".[\[72\]](#cite%5Fnote-72)[\[73\]](#cite%5Fnote-73)

#### Iranian Revolution

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Iran%E2%80%93United%5FStates%5Frelations&action=edit&section=14%20%22Edit%20section:%20Iranian%20Revolution%22)\]

Further information: [Iranian Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian%5FRevolution "Iranian Revolution")

The Iranian/Islamic Revolution (1978–1979) ousted the Shah and replaced him with the anti-American Supreme Leader [Ayatollah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayatollah "Ayatollah") [Ruhollah Khomeini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruhollah%5FKhomeini "Ruhollah Khomeini").[\[74\]](#cite%5Fnote-74) The United States government State Department and intelligence services "consistently underestimated the magnitude and long-term implications of this unrest".[\[75\]](#cite%5Fnote-75) Six months before the revolution culminated, the CIA had produced a report stating that "Iran is not in a revolutionary or even a 'prerevolutionary' situation."[\[76\]](#cite%5Fnote-76)[\[77\]](#cite%5Fnote-77)

Revolutionary students feared the power of the United States, particularly the CIA, to overthrow a new Iranian government. One source of this concern was a book by CIA agent [Kermit Roosevelt Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kermit%5FRoosevelt%5FJr. "Kermit Roosevelt Jr.") titled _Countercoup: The Struggle for Control of Iran_. Many students had read excerpts from the book and thought that the CIA would attempt to implement this countercoup strategy.[\[78\]](#cite%5Fnote-78)

Khomeini referred to America as the "[Great Satan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great%5FSatan "Great Satan")"[\[79\]](#cite%5Fnote-79) and instantly got rid of the Shah's prime minister, replacing him with politician [Mehdi Bazargan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mehdi%5FBazargan "Mehdi Bazargan"). Until this point, the Carter administration was still hoping for normal relationships with Iran, sending its National Security Adviser Zbigniew Brzezinski.

The Islamic revolutionaries wished to extradite and execute the ousted Shah, and Carter refused to give him any further support or help return him to power. The Shah, suffering from terminal cancer, requested entry into the United States for treatment. The American embassy in Tehran opposed the request, as they were intent on stabilizing relations between the new interim revolutionary government of Iran and the United States.[\[67\]](#cite%5Fnote-JC-67) However, President Carter agreed to let the Shah in after pressure from [Henry Kissinger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry%5FKissinger "Henry Kissinger"), [David Rockefeller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David%5FRockefeller "David Rockefeller"), and other pro-Shah political figures. Iranians' suspicion that the Shah was actually trying to conspire against the Iranian Revolution grew; thus, this incident was often used by the Iranian revolutionaries to justify their claims that the former monarch was an American puppet, and this led to the storming of the American embassy by radical students allied with Khomeini.[\[67\]](#cite%5Fnote-JC-67)

#### The hostage crisis and its consequences

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Iran%E2%80%93United%5FStates%5Frelations&action=edit&section=15%20%22Edit%20section:%20The%20hostage%20crisis%20and%20its%20consequences%22)\]

Main article: [Iran hostage crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%5Fhostage%5Fcrisis "Iran hostage crisis")

[](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:DF-SC-82-06566%5FBush%5Fhostages%5FIran.JPEG)

Vice President [George H. W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George%5FH.%5FW.%5FBush "George H. W. Bush") and other VIPs wait to welcome the former hostages to Iran home

On November 4, 1979, Iranian student revolutionaries, with [Ayatollah Khomeini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruhollah%5FKhomeini "Ruhollah Khomeini")'s approval, seized the [U.S. embassy in Tehran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Embassy%5Fof%5Fthe%5FUnited%5FStates,%5FTehran "Embassy of the United States, Tehran"), holding 52 American diplomats hostage for 444 days in response to the U.S. granting asylum to the deposed Shah. The crisis, seen in Iran as a stand against American influence and in the U.S. as a violation of diplomatic law, led to failed rescue attempts and lasting damage to Iran–U.S. relations. Six Americans escaped via the CIA–Canadian "[Canadian Caper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canadian%5FCaper "Canadian Caper")" operation, later dramatized in the film _[Argo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Argo%5F%282012%5Ffilm%29 "Argo (2012 film)")_.   
As a response to the seizure of the embassy, Carter banned Iranian oil imports,[\[80\]](#cite%5Fnote-iranpri-80) followed by his [Executive Order 12170](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executive%5FOrder%5F12170 "Executive Order 12170") froze about $12 billion in Iranian assets,[\[81\]](#cite%5Fnote-81) including bank deposits, gold and other properties. They were the first of a number of [international sanctions against Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International%5Fsanctions%5Fagainst%5FIran "International sanctions against Iran").[\[82\]](#cite%5Fnote-scholar.harvard.edu-82)

[](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Boeing%5FVC-137%5F54.jpg)

Families wait for the former hostages to disembark the plane

The crisis ended with the [Algiers Accords](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algiers%5FAccords%5F%281981%29 "Algiers Accords (1981)") in January 1981\. Under the terms of the agreement and Iran's compliance, the hostaged diplomats were allowed to leave Iran. One of the chief provisions of the Accords was that the United States would lift the freeze on Iranian assets and remove trade sanctions.[\[83\]](#cite%5Fnote-83) The agreement also established the [Iran–U.S. Claims Tribunal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran–United%5FStates%5FClaims%5FTribunal "Iran–United States Claims Tribunal") in The Hague to handle claims brought by Americans against Iran, as well as claims by Iran against Americans and the former shah.[\[80\]](#cite%5Fnote-iranpri-80) The diplomatic ties remain severed, with [Switzerland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland "Switzerland") and [Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interests%5FSection%5Fof%5FIran%5Fin%5Fthe%5FUnited%5FStates "Interests Section of Iran in the United States") handling each country's interests.

### Reagan administration 1981–1989

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Iran%E2%80%93United%5FStates%5Frelations&action=edit&section=16%20%22Edit%20section:%20Reagan%20administration%201981%E2%80%931989%22)\]

#### Iran–Iraq War

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Iran%E2%80%93United%5FStates%5Frelations&action=edit&section=17%20%22Edit%20section:%20Iran%E2%80%93Iraq%20War%22)\]

See also: [United States support for Iraq during the Iran–Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United%5FStates%5Fsupport%5Ffor%5FIraq%5Fduring%5Fthe%5FIran–Iraq%5FWar "United States support for Iraq during the Iran–Iraq War")

American intelligence and logistical support played a crucial role in arming Iraq in the [Iran–Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran–Iraq%5FWar "Iran–Iraq War"). However, [Bob Woodward](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bob%5FWoodward "Bob Woodward") states that the United States gave information to both sides, hoping "to engineer a stalemate".[\[84\]](#cite%5Fnote-84) In search for a new set or order in this region, Washington adopted a policy designed to contain both sides economically and militarily.[\[85\]](#cite%5Fnote-Simbar%5F2006%5F73–87-85) During the second half of the Iran–Iraq War, the [Reagan administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency%5Fof%5FRonald%5FReagan "Presidency of Ronald Reagan") pursued several sanction bills against Iran; on the other hand, it established full diplomatic relations with [Saddam Hussein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saddam%5FHussein "Saddam Hussein")'s [Ba'athist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ba'athist%5FIraq "Ba'athist Iraq") government in [Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraq "Iraq") by removing it from the U.S. list of [State Sponsors of Terrorism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State%5FSponsors%5Fof%5FTerrorism "State Sponsors of Terrorism") in 1984.[\[85\]](#cite%5Fnote-Simbar%5F2006%5F73–87-85) According to the [U.S. Senate Banking Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S.%5FSenate%5FBanking%5FCommittee "U.S. Senate Banking Committee"), the administrations of Presidents Reagan and [George H. W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George%5FH.%5FW.%5FBush "George H. W. Bush") authorized the sale to Iraq of numerous [dual-use](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual-use%5Ftechnology "Dual-use technology") items, including chemicals and biological agents, such as [anthrax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthrax "Anthrax") and [bubonic plague](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bubonic%5Fplague "Bubonic plague").[\[86\]](#cite%5Fnote-86) The Iran–Iraq War ended with both agreeing to a ceasefire in 1988.

#### 1983: Hezbollah bombings

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Iran%E2%80%93United%5FStates%5Frelations&action=edit&section=18%20%22Edit%20section:%201983:%20Hezbollah%20bombings%22)\]

Main articles: [Hezbollah military activities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hezbollah%5Fmilitary%5Factivities "Hezbollah military activities"), [1983 US embassy bombing in Beirut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1983%5FUS%5Fembassy%5Fbombing%5Fin%5FBeirut "1983 US embassy bombing in Beirut"), and [1983 Beirut barracks bombings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1983%5FBeirut%5Fbarracks%5Fbombings "1983 Beirut barracks bombings")

[Hezbollah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hezbollah "Hezbollah"), an Iran-backed [Shi'ite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shia%5FIslam "Shia Islam") Islamist group, has carried out multiple anti-American attacks, including the 1983 U.S. Embassy bombing in [Beirut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beirut "Beirut") (killing 63, including 17 Americans), the Beirut barracks bombing (killing 241 U.S. Marines), and the 1996 [Khobar Towers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khobar%5FTowers%5Fbombing "Khobar Towers bombing") bombing. U.S. courts have ruled Iran responsible for these attacks, with evidence showing Hezbollah operated under Iran's direction and that [Supreme Leader Ali Khamenei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali%5FKhamenei "Ali Khamenei") authorized the Khobar Towers bombing.

#### 1983: Anti-communist purge

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Iran%E2%80%93United%5FStates%5Frelations&action=edit&section=19%20%22Edit%20section:%201983:%20Anti-communist%20purge%22)\]

According to the [Tower Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tower%5FCommission "Tower Commission") report:

> In 1983, the U.S. helped bring to the attention of Tehran the threat inherent in the extensive infiltration of the government by the communist [Tudeh Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tudeh%5FParty%5Fof%5FIran "Tudeh Party of Iran") and Soviet or pro-Soviet cadres in the country. Using this information, the Khomeini government took measures, including mass executions, that virtually eliminated the pro-Soviet infrastructure in Iran.[\[87\]](#cite%5Fnote-87)

#### Iran–Contra Affair

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Iran%E2%80%93United%5FStates%5Frelations&action=edit&section=20%20%22Edit%20section:%20Iran%E2%80%93Contra%20Affair%22)\]

Further information: [Iran–Contra affair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran–Contra%5Faffair "Iran–Contra affair")

To evade congressional rules regarding an arms embargo, officials in [President Ronald Reagan's administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency%5Fof%5FRonald%5FReagan "Presidency of Ronald Reagan") arranged in the mid-1980s to sell arms to Iran in an attempt to improve relations and obtain their influence in the release of hostages held in Lebanon. [Oliver North](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oliver%5FNorth "Oliver North") of the [National Security Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United%5FStates%5FNational%5FSecurity%5FCouncil "United States National Security Council") diverted proceeds from the arms sale to fund anti-Marxist [Contra rebels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contras "Contras") in [Nicaragua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicaragua "Nicaragua").[\[88\]](#cite%5Fnote-88)[\[89\]](#cite%5Fnote-reaganspeech-89) In November 1986, Reagan issued a statement denying the arms sales.[\[90\]](#cite%5Fnote-90) One week later, he confirmed that weapons had been transferred to Iran, but denied that they were part of an exchange for hostages.[\[89\]](#cite%5Fnote-reaganspeech-89) Later investigations by Congress and an independent counsel disclosed details of both operations and noted that documents relating to the affair were destroyed or withheld from investigators by Reagan administration officials on national security grounds.[\[91\]](#cite%5Fnote-Excerpts%5Ffrom%5FIran-Contra%5FReport-91)[\[92\]](#cite%5Fnote-92) The revelation that profits from the arms sales had been illegally funneled to the Contras created a major political scandal for Reagan.[\[38\]](#cite%5Fnote-briefhis-38)

#### United States attack of 1988

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Iran%E2%80%93United%5FStates%5Frelations&action=edit&section=21%20%22Edit%20section:%20United%20States%20attack%20of%201988%22)\]

Main article: [Iran–Iraq War § Attacks on shipping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran–Iraq%5FWar#Attacks%5Fon%5Fshipping "Iran–Iraq War")

In 1988, the U.S. launched [Operation Praying Mantis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation%5FPraying%5FMantis "Operation Praying Mantis") in retaliation for Iran mining the [Persian Gulf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian%5FGulf "Persian Gulf") during the Iran–Iraq War, following [Operation Nimble Archer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation%5FNimble%5FArcher "Operation Nimble Archer"). It was the largest American naval operation since [World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World%5FWar%5FII "World War II"), with strikes that destroyed two Iranian oil platforms and sank a major warship. Iran sought reparations at the [International Court of Justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International%5FCourt%5Fof%5FJustice "International Court of Justice"), but the court dismissed the claim. The attack helped pressure Iran into agreeing to a ceasefire with Iraq later that year.

#### 1988: Iran Air Flight 655

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Iran%E2%80%93United%5FStates%5Frelations&action=edit&section=22%20%22Edit%20section:%201988:%20Iran%20Air%20Flight%20655%22)\]

Main article: [Iran Air Flight 655](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%5FAir%5FFlight%5F655 "Iran Air Flight 655")

On July 3, 1988, during the [Iran–Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran–Iraq%5FWar "Iran–Iraq War"), the U.S. Navy's [USS _Vincennes_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS%5FVincennes%5F%28CG-49%29 "USS Vincennes (CG-49)") mistakenly shot down [Iran Air Flight 655](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%5FAir%5FFlight%5F655 "Iran Air Flight 655"), a civilian [Airbus A300B2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airbus%5FA300 "Airbus A300"), killing 290 people.[\[38\]](#cite%5Fnote-briefhis-38) The U.S. initially claimed the aircraft was a warplane and outside the civilian air corridor, but later acknowledged the downing was an accident in a combat zone. Iran, however, argued it was gross negligence and sued the U.S. in the International Court of Justice, resulting in compensation for the victims' families. The U.S. expressed regret, calling it a tragic accident, while the _Vincennes_ crew received military honors.

### George H. W. Bush administration 1989–1993

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Iran%E2%80%93United%5FStates%5Frelations&action=edit&section=23%20%22Edit%20section:%20George%20H.%20W.%20Bush%20administration%201989%E2%80%931993%22)\]

Newly elected U.S. president [George H. W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency%5Fof%5FGeorge%5FH.%5FW.%5FBush "Presidency of George H. W. Bush") announced a "goodwill begets goodwill" gesture in his inaugural speech on January 20, 1989\. The Bush administration urged [President of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President%5Fof%5FIran "President of Iran") [Akbar Hashemi Rafsanjani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akbar%5FHashemi%5FRafsanjani "Akbar Hashemi Rafsanjani") to use Iran's influence in Lebanon to obtain the release of the remaining [U.S. hostages held by Hezbollah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lebanon%5Fhostage%5Fcrisis "Lebanon hostage crisis"). Bush indicated there would be a reciprocal gesture toward Iran by the United States.[\[93\]](#cite%5Fnote-93)

Relevant background events during the first year of Bush's administration include the ending of the [Iran–Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran–Iraq%5FWar "Iran–Iraq War") and the death of [Ayatollah Khomeini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayatollah%5FKhomeini "Ayatollah Khomeini").[\[24\]](#cite%5Fnote-delv-24) Khomeini believed he had a sacred duty to purge Iran of what he saw as Western corruption and moral decay, aiming to restore the country to religious purity under Islamic theocratic rule.[\[24\]](#cite%5Fnote-delv-24) Khomeini was succeeded by [Ali Khamenei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali%5FKhamenei "Ali Khamenei").

### Clinton administration 1993–2001

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Iran%E2%80%93United%5FStates%5Frelations&action=edit&section=24%20%22Edit%20section:%20Clinton%20administration%201993%E2%80%932001%22)\]

See also: [Dual containment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual%5Fcontainment "Dual containment")

In April 1995, a total oil and trade [embargo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Embargo "Embargo") on dealings with Iran by American companies was imposed by [Bill Clinton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency%5Fof%5FBill%5FClinton "Presidency of Bill Clinton").[\[28\]](#cite%5Fnote-weisse-28) This ended trade, which had been growing following the end of the Iran–Iraq War.[\[94\]](#cite%5Fnote-94) The next year, the American Congress passed the Iran-Libya Sanctions act, designed to prevent other countries from making large investments in Iranian energy. The act was denounced by the European CC as invalid.[\[95\]](#cite%5Fnote-95)

#### Khatami and Iranian reformers

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Iran%E2%80%93United%5FStates%5Frelations&action=edit&section=25%20%22Edit%20section:%20Khatami%20and%20Iranian%20reformers%22)\]

In January 1998, newly elected Iranian President [Mohammad Khatami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad%5FKhatami "Mohammad Khatami") called for a "dialogue of civilizations" with the United States. In the interview, Khatami invoked [Alexis de Tocqueville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexis%5Fde%5FTocqueville "Alexis de Tocqueville")'s _[Democracy in America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democracy%5Fin%5FAmerica "Democracy in America")_ to explain similarities between American and Iranian quests for freedom.[\[96\]](#cite%5Fnote-96) American Secretary of State [Madeleine Albright](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madeleine%5FAlbright "Madeleine Albright") responded positively. This brought free travel between the countries as well as an end to the American embargo of [Persian carpets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian%5Fcarpet "Persian carpet") and [pistachios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pistachio "Pistachio"). Relations then stalled due to opposition from [Iranian conservatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian%5FConservatives "Iranian Conservatives") and American preconditions for discussions, including changes in [Iranian policy on Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran–Israel%5Frelations "Iran–Israel relations"), nuclear energy, and [support for terrorism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%5Fand%5Fstate-sponsored%5Fterrorism "Iran and state-sponsored terrorism").[\[97\]](#cite%5Fnote-97)[\[98\]](#cite%5Fnote-98)

Four members of the United States Congress: Senator [Arlen Specter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arlen%5FSpecter "Arlen Specter"), Representative [Bob Ney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bob%5FNey "Bob Ney"), Representative [Gary Ackerman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gary%5FAckerman "Gary Ackerman"), and Representative [Eliot L. Engel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eliot%5FL.%5FEngel "Eliot L. Engel") held informal talks in [New York City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New%5FYork%5FCity "New York City") with several Iranian leaders in August 2000\. The Iranians included [Mehdi Karroubi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mehdi%5FKarroubi "Mehdi Karroubi"), speaker of the [Majlis of Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majlis%5Fof%5FIran "Majlis of Iran") (Iranian Parliament); [Maurice Motamed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maurice%5FMotamed "Maurice Motamed"), a [Jewish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History%5Fof%5Fthe%5FJews%5Fin%5FIran "History of the Jews in Iran") member of the Majlis; and three other Iranian parliamentarians.[\[99\]](#cite%5Fnote-99)

### George W. Bush administration 2001–2009

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Iran%E2%80%93United%5FStates%5Frelations&action=edit&section=26%20%22Edit%20section:%20George%20W.%20Bush%20administration%202001%E2%80%932009%22)\]

Main article: [Iran–United States relations during the George W. Bush administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran–United%5FStates%5Frelations%5Fduring%5Fthe%5FGeorge%5FW.%5FBush%5Fadministration "Iran–United States relations during the George W. Bush administration")

Iran–United States relations during the George W. Bush administration (2001–2009) were marked by heightened tensions, mutual distrust, and periodic attempts at limited engagement. Following the [September 11 attacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/September%5F11%5Fattacks "September 11 attacks") in 2001, Iran initially was sympathetic with the United States.[\[100\]](#cite%5Fnote-president-100)

Relations deteriorated sharply after President [George W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George%5FW.%5FBush "George W. Bush") labeled Iran part of the "[Axis of Evil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Axis%5Fof%5FEvil "Axis of Evil")" in 2002, accusing the country of pursuing weapons of mass destruction that posed a threat to the U.S.[\[101\]](#cite%5Fnote-suterror-101)[\[102\]](#cite%5Fnote-fax-102) In 2003, Swiss Ambassador Tim Guldimann relayed an unofficial proposal to the U.S. outlining a possible "grand bargain" with Iran. He claimed it was developed in cooperation with Iran, but it lacked formal Iranian endorsement, and the Bush administration did not pursue the offer.[\[103\]](#cite%5Fnote-Guldi-103)[\[104\]](#cite%5Fnote-Kessler-104)[\[105\]](#cite%5Fnote-Rubin-105)

Between 2003 and 2008, Iran accused the United States of repeatedly violating its territorial sovereignty through drone incursions,[\[106\]](#cite%5Fnote-washdrones-106)[\[107\]](#cite%5Fnote-nytdronesresponse-107) covert operations, and support for opposition groups.[\[108\]](#cite%5Fnote-seymourhershone-108)[\[109\]](#cite%5Fnote-kucinich-109) In August 2005, [Mahmoud Ahmadinejad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahmoud%5FAhmadinejad "Mahmoud Ahmadinejad") became Iran's president. During his presidency, attempts at dialogue, including a [personal letter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahmoud%5FAhmadinejad's%5Fletter%5Fto%5FGeorge%5FW.%5FBush "Mahmoud Ahmadinejad's letter to George W. Bush") to President Bush,[\[110\]](#cite%5Fnote-Ahmaletter-110) were dismissed by U.S. officials,[\[111\]](#cite%5Fnote-WPLetter-111) while public tensions grew over Iran's nuclear program, U.S. foreign policy, and Ahmadinejad's controversial remarks at international forums. The United States intensified covert operations against Iran, including alleged support for militant groups such as [PEJAK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PEJAK "PEJAK") and [Jundullah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jundallah%5F%28Iran%29 "Jundallah (Iran)"), cross-border activities, and expanded CIA and Special Forces missions. Iran was repeatedly accused by the U.S. of arming and training Iraqi insurgents, including [Shiite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shiite "Shiite") militias and groups linked to [Hezbollah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hezbollah "Hezbollah"), with American officials citing captured weapons, satellite images, and detainee testimonies. During this period, additional flashpoints included the U.S. raid on Iran's consulate in [Erbil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erbil "Erbil"), sanctions targeting Iranian financial institutions, a naval dispute in the [Strait of Hormuz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strait%5Fof%5FHormuz "Strait of Hormuz"), and the public disclosure of covert action plans against Iran.[\[112\]](#cite%5Fnote-112)

### Obama administration 2009–2017

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Iran%E2%80%93United%5FStates%5Frelations&action=edit&section=27%20%22Edit%20section:%20Obama%20administration%202009%E2%80%932017%22)\]

Main article: [Iran–United States relations during the Obama administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran–United%5FStates%5Frelations%5Fduring%5Fthe%5FObama%5Fadministration "Iran–United States relations during the Obama administration")

[](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Secretary%5FKerry%5Fand%5FIranian%5FForeign%5FMinister%5FZarif%5FPose%5Ffor%5Fa%5FPhoto%5Fat%5Fthe%5FUnited%5FNations%5Fin%5FNew%5FYork%5FCity%5F%2821543582838%29.jpg)

U.S. Secretary of State [John Kerry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John%5FKerry "John Kerry") and Iranian Foreign Minister [Mohammad Javad Zarif](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammad%5FJavad%5FZarif "Mohammad Javad Zarif") in [Lausanne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lausanne "Lausanne"), Switzerland to discuss the [Iranian nuclear program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint%5FComprehensive%5FPlan%5Fof%5FAction "Joint Comprehensive Plan of Action"), March 16, 2015

[](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Secretary%5FKerry%5FShakes%5FHands%5FWith%5FIranian%5FForeign%5FMinister%5FZarif%5FBefore%5FTheir%5FMeeting%5Fat%5Fthe%5FUnited%5FNations%5Fin%5FNew%5FYork%5F%2826461605021%29.jpg)

Secretary of State John Kerry shakes hands with Iranian Foreign Minister Javad Zarif at the UN Headquarters in New York on April 19, 2016

Iran–United States relations during the Obama administration (2009–2017) were defined by a shift from confrontation to cautious engagement, culminating in the landmark nuclear agreement of 2015.

At the start of [Obama's presidency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency%5Fof%5FBarack%5FObama "Presidency of Barack Obama"), both sides exchanged public messages signaling a possible thaw, with Iran voicing long-standing grievances[\[113\]](#cite%5Fnote-npr%5Fhalfway-113) and the United States calling for mutual respect and responsibility.[\[114\]](#cite%5Fnote-newbegin-114) However, after [Mahmoud Ahmadinejad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahmoud%5FAhmadinejad "Mahmoud Ahmadinejad")'s disputed re-election in 2009, which sparked mass protests and allegations of fraud, the United States responded with skepticism and concern.[\[115\]](#cite%5Fnote-skep-115) In late 2011 and early 2012, [Iran threatened to close the Strait of Hormuz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011–2012%5FStrait%5Fof%5FHormuz%5Fdispute "2011–2012 Strait of Hormuz dispute")[\[116\]](#cite%5Fnote-Hormuz11-116) and warned a U.S. aircraft carrier not to return to the Persian Gulf. The U.S. rejected the warning and maintained its naval presence, while experts doubted Iran's ability to sustain a blockade.[\[117\]](#cite%5Fnote-Cordesman-117)[\[118\]](#cite%5Fnote-Talmadge-118)

The 2013 election of President [Hassan Rouhani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hassan%5FRouhani "Hassan Rouhani"), seen as a moderate,[\[119\]](#cite%5Fnote-moderate-119) marked a shift in tone, with his outreach at the UN and a historic [phone call with Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phone%5Fconversation%5Fbetween%5FBarack%5FObama%5Fand%5FHassan%5FRouhani "Phone conversation between Barack Obama and Hassan Rouhani") signaling renewed diplomatic engagement.[\[120\]](#cite%5Fnote-call-120)[\[121\]](#cite%5Fnote-121) While high-level contact resumed and symbolic gestures were exchanged, conservative backlash in Iran highlighted internal divisions over rapprochement.[\[122\]](#cite%5Fnote-protests-122) In 2015, the United States and other world powers reached the [Joint Comprehensive Plan of Action](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint%5FComprehensive%5FPlan%5Fof%5FAction "Joint Comprehensive Plan of Action") (JCPOA) with Iran, under which Iran agreed to limit its nuclear program in exchange for sanctions relief.[\[123\]](#cite%5Fnote-deal-123) The agreement marked a major diplomatic achievement for the Obama administration, though it faced skepticism in Congress[\[124\]](#cite%5Fnote-LoBianco%5FTatum%5F2015-124) and mixed public support in the U.S.

Despite the JCPOA, tensions between the United States and Iran persisted over ballistic missile tests, continued U.S. sanctions, and European business hesitancy due to fear of U.S. penalties.[\[125\]](#cite%5Fnote-nucdeal-125)[\[126\]](#cite%5Fnote-summana-126)[\[127\]](#cite%5Fnote-EUblock-127)[\[128\]](#cite%5Fnote-DispRes-128) The administration also faced criticism for its handling of these issues, both from Iran[\[129\]](#cite%5Fnote-IranImp-129) and from political opponents.

### First Trump administration 2017–2021

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Iran%E2%80%93United%5FStates%5Frelations&action=edit&section=28%20%22Edit%20section:%20First%20Trump%20administration%202017%E2%80%932021%22)\]

Main article: [Iran–United States relations during the first Trump administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran–United%5FStates%5Frelations%5Fduring%5Fthe%5Ffirst%5FTrump%5Fadministration "Iran–United States relations during the first Trump administration")

Iran–United States relations during the first Trump administration (2017–2021) were marked by a sharp policy shift from [Obama's engagement-oriented approach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran–United%5FStates%5Frelations%5Fduring%5Fthe%5FObama%5Fadministration "Iran–United States relations during the Obama administration"). Trump began with a [travel ban affecting Iranian citizens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executive%5FOrder%5F13769 "Executive Order 13769"),[\[130\]](#cite%5Fnote-travelban-130) and [withdrew from the Joint Comprehensive Plan of Action](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United%5FStates%5Fwithdrawal%5Ffrom%5Fthe%5FJoint%5FComprehensive%5FPlan%5Fof%5FAction "United States withdrawal from the Joint Comprehensive Plan of Action") [(JCPOA)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint%5FComprehensive%5FPlan%5Fof%5FAction "Joint Comprehensive Plan of Action"). A broader [maximum pressure campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maximum%5Fpressure%5Fcampaign "Maximum pressure campaign") followed, with over 1,500 sanctions targeting Iran's financial, oil, and shipping sectors, as well as foreign firms doing business with Iran, severely damaging its economy.[\[131\]](#cite%5Fnote-131) The effort aimed to isolate Iran but met with strong resistance—even from U.S. allies—and often left Washington diplomatically isolated.[\[132\]](#cite%5Fnote-Laraj-132)

Iran responded by threatening to resume unrestricted uranium enrichment;[\[133\]](#cite%5Fnote-limit-133) rejecting negotiations with the Trump administration,[\[134\]](#cite%5Fnote-nowar-134) and intensified rhetoric. Tensions escalated in 2019 with U.S. intelligence reports of Iranian threats,[\[135\]](#cite%5Fnote-explained-135)[\[136\]](#cite%5Fnote-strike-136) attacks on oil tankers, the [downing of a U.S. drone by Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019%5FIranian%5Fshoot-down%5Fof%5FAmerican%5Fdrone "2019 Iranian shoot-down of American drone"),[\[137\]](#cite%5Fnote-shotdrone-137) and suspected Iranian strikes on Saudi oil facilities.[\[138\]](#cite%5Fnote-oilwar-138) President Trump called off retaliatory strikes,[\[139\]](#cite%5Fnote-NYTCocked-139) opting for cyberattacks and additional sanctions instead.[\[140\]](#cite%5Fnote-cyber-140)

[](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Demonstrations%5Fand%5Fprotests%5Fagainst%5FUnited%5FStates%5Frecognition%5Fof%5FJerusalem%5Fin%5FTehran%5F036.jpg)

Iranians protest Donald Trump's [Jerusalem declaration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United%5FStates%5Frecognition%5Fof%5FJerusalem%5Fas%5Fcapital%5Fof%5FIsrael "United States recognition of Jerusalem as capital of Israel") in Tehran, December 11, 2017

A major escalation followed a December 2019 [rocket attack on the K-1 Air Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019%5FK-1%5FAir%5FBase%5Fattack "2019 K-1 Air Base attack") in Iraq,[\[141\]](#cite%5Fnote-killedcont-141) which led to American airstrikes on Iranian-backed militias[\[142\]](#cite%5Fnote-BRubin-142) and a retaliatory [attack on the U.S. embassy in Baghdad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attack%5Fon%5Fthe%5FUnited%5FStates%5Fembassy%5Fin%5FBaghdad "Attack on the United States embassy in Baghdad").[\[143\]](#cite%5Fnote-BEmbassy-143) On January 3, 2020, the U.S. [assassinated Iranian General Qasem Soleimani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assassination%5Fof%5FQasem%5FSoleimani "Assassination of Qasem Soleimani") in a drone strike,[\[144\]](#cite%5Fnote-slain-144) prompting Iranian missile attacks on U.S. bases in Iraq and heightened fears of war. The crisis deepened with the accidental [downing of a Ukrainian passenger plane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukraine%5FInternational%5FAirlines%5FFlight%5F752 "Ukraine International Airlines Flight 752") by Iranian forces and continued through early 2020 with retaliatory strikes and threats.[\[145\]](#cite%5Fnote-RFE-145)

Later in 2020, Iran blamed U.S. sanctions for limiting its COVID-19 response.[\[146\]](#cite%5Fnote-irancovid-146) They launched a military satellite,[\[147\]](#cite%5Fnote-rocket-147) and were later accused of interfering in the U.S. presidential election[\[148\]](#cite%5Fnote-election-148) and proxy attacks.[\[149\]](#cite%5Fnote-cnn-taliban-149) Relations ended under Trump with continued hostility and unresolved disputes.

### Biden administration 2021–2025

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Iran%E2%80%93United%5FStates%5Frelations&action=edit&section=29%20%22Edit%20section:%20Biden%20administration%202021%E2%80%932025%22)\]

Main article: [Iran–United States relations during the Biden administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran–United%5FStates%5Frelations%5Fduring%5Fthe%5FBiden%5Fadministration "Iran–United States relations during the Biden administration")

Iran–United States relations during the Biden administration (2021–2025) were shaped by efforts to revive the 2015 nuclear agreement alongside ongoing regional tensions, sanctions, cyberattacks, and proxy conflicts. Early in Joe Biden's presidency, U.S. officials expressed interest in returning to the [Joint Comprehensive Plan of Action](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint%5FComprehensive%5FPlan%5Fof%5FAction "Joint Comprehensive Plan of Action") (JCPOA),[\[150\]](#cite%5Fnote-blunder-150) but negotiations in Vienna eventually stalled.[\[151\]](#cite%5Fnote-1953-2023%5Ftimeline-151)[\[152\]](#cite%5Fnote-stalling-152) [Iran increased uranium enrichment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear%5Fprogram%5Fof%5FIran "Nuclear program of Iran") and imposed retaliatory sanctions,[\[151\]](#cite%5Fnote-1953-2023%5Ftimeline-151)[\[153\]](#cite%5Fnote-15sanct-153) while the U.S. imposed new sanctions over missile programs, oil exports, and human rights abuses.[\[154\]](#cite%5Fnote-Blinkdeal-154)[\[155\]](#cite%5Fnote-Moscsanct-155)[\[156\]](#cite%5Fnote-IranChina-156)[\[157\]](#cite%5Fnote-157)[\[154\]](#cite%5Fnote-Blinkdeal-154)[\[155\]](#cite%5Fnote-Moscsanct-155)[\[156\]](#cite%5Fnote-IranChina-156)

Tensions persisted throughout this era, marked by recurring proxy attacks on U.S. bases,[\[158\]](#cite%5Fnote-Kataib-158) which intensified following the outbreak of the Gaza war in late 2023,[\[159\]](#cite%5Fnote-proiran-159) and by subsequent American retaliatory strikes.[\[160\]](#cite%5Fnote-65milit-160) The period also saw disputes over the [assassination of Qasem Soleimani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assassination%5Fof%5FQasem%5FSoleimani "Assassination of Qasem Soleimani"),[\[161\]](#cite%5Fnote-Motamedi-161)[\[162\]](#cite%5Fnote-avengeUS-162)[\[163\]](#cite%5Fnote-offercon-163) and military escalations across the Gulf region.[\[164\]](#cite%5Fnote-ErbilUS-164)[\[165\]](#cite%5Fnote-Iranseizes-165)[\[166\]](#cite%5Fnote-1000US-166) In 2023, a breakthrough occurred with a U.S.–Iran prisoner swap and the release of frozen Iranian funds,[\[167\]](#cite%5Fnote-6Bdeal-167)[\[168\]](#cite%5Fnote-humani-168)[\[169\]](#cite%5Fnote-breakth-169) though indirect diplomacy remained fragile.[\[170\]](#cite%5Fnote-EUhoped-170) Iran was later accused of interfering in the 2024 U.S. presidential election through cyber operations and AI disinformation.[\[171\]](#cite%5Fnote-Iranhack-171)[\[172\]](#cite%5Fnote-Iranmail-172)[\[173\]](#cite%5Fnote-AI3lines-173) Alleged assassination plots targeting Donald Trump and dissidents on U.S. soil further strained relations.[\[174\]](#cite%5Fnote-KD-174) By late 2024, relations remained adversarial, marked by unresolved security disputes and growing mistrust.

### Second Trump administration 2025–2026

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Iran%E2%80%93United%5FStates%5Frelations&action=edit&section=30%20%22Edit%20section:%20Second%20Trump%20administration%202025%E2%80%932026%22)\]

Main article: [Iran–United States relations during the second Trump administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran–United%5FStates%5Frelations%5Fduring%5Fthe%5Fsecond%5FTrump%5Fadministration "Iran–United States relations during the second Trump administration")

In November 2024, the [U.S. Department of Justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S.%5FDepartment%5Fof%5FJustice "U.S. Department of Justice") alleged that Iran has attempted to assassinate U.S. officials and dissidents, including Trump.[\[175\]](#cite%5Fnote-JD-175)[\[174\]](#cite%5Fnote-KD-174) In February 2025, three weeks after [his second inauguration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second%5Finauguration%5Fof%5FDonald%5FTrump "Second inauguration of Donald Trump"), Trump said he had given the U.S. military and his advisors instructions to obliterate Iran if he were to be assassinated.[\[176\]](#cite%5Fnote-176)

In February, Trump signed the return of the [Maximum pressure campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maximum%5Fpressure%5Fcampaign "Maximum pressure campaign") against the Iranian government.[\[177\]](#cite%5Fnote-177)[\[178\]](#cite%5Fnote-178)

Trump called for talks for a nuclear peace agreement.[\[179\]](#cite%5Fnote-179)

In the [Friday prayer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Friday%5Fprayer "Friday prayer") of February 7, Khamenei dismissed negotiations and stated that the Iranian government should not make a deal with the U.S.[\[180\]](#cite%5Fnote-180) February 9, Trump said that he would rather make a deal with Iranians than let them be bombed by Israel.[\[181\]](#cite%5Fnote-181)[\[182\]](#cite%5Fnote-182)

In March 2025, Khamenei stated that he did not intend to negotiate with Trump.[\[183\]](#cite%5Fnote-183) IRGC General Salami threatened United States military with devastation.[\[184\]](#cite%5Fnote-184) Trump threatened he would hold Iranian regime to blame for any shots [fired by Houthis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houthi%5Fattacks%5Fon%5Fcommercial%5Fvessels "Houthi attacks on commercial vessels").[\[185\]](#cite%5Fnote-185)

[Putin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Putin "Putin") and Trump reached an agreement that Iran should never be in a position to destroy Israel.[\[186\]](#cite%5Fnote-186)

In April, [Ali Larijani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali%5FLarijani "Ali Larijani"), advisor to Khamenei, threatened Trump that Iran would make nuclear weapons.[\[187\]](#cite%5Fnote-187) Islamic Republic military allegedly had recommended a preemptively strike on U.S. military bases.[\[188\]](#cite%5Fnote-188)
